macOS 27.3 adds native Markdown rendering to Quick Look

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Starting with macOS 27.3, users can preview Markdown files with proper formatting directly in Quick Look. Here are the details.

Apple released macOS 27.3 beta 1 today, with new Safari features, including Vertical Tabs and Adaptive Dark Mode, expanded Siri AI capabilities, and new tools for third-party developers.

Among the smaller changes coming to the Mac is a welcome improvement to Quick Look. As of macOS 27.3 beta 1, .md files are rendered with proper formatting directly in Quick Look, rather than displaying their raw Markdown syntax.

Apple has been expanding Markdown support across its platforms, including the ability to import and export Markdown files in Notes. Markdown has also become increasingly important with the rise of AI agents, which use .md files to store instructions, context, and other information.

In fact, as Federico Viticci noted today on X, Apple’s new Siri AI app skills feature also uses Markdown-based SKILL.md files, allowing developers to provide instructions that help Siri perform complex tasks within their apps.

What a journey for Markdown, @gruber The new Siri AI developer framework now officially uses skills, with specific Markdown guidance for SKILL .md agent files https://t.co/koi8K541le pic.twitter.com/TF72JJZRXc

For the uninitiated, Markdown is a simple text formatting syntax created by John Gruber in 2004, and has since become widely used for writing documentation, publishing content online (including this very post), and, more recently, providing instructions to AI agents.

Markdown is a text-to-HTML conversion tool for web writers. Markdown allows you to write using an easy-to-read, easy-to-write plain text format, then convert it to structurally valid HTML.

In practice, this means you can create headings, bold text, lists, and links using just a few simple characters. And, starting with macOS 27.3, you’ll be able to preview the results directly in Quick Look, without having to open the file in a separate app.

To learn more about Markdown, follow this link.

Get Out and Stay Out with These All-Weather Layers

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Some of the best holiday gifts have a story behind them. With Berghaus, that story stretches back six decades, to 1966. That’s when mountaineers Gordon Davison and Peter Lockey founded the brand and began creating innovative, stylish outdoor apparel from their headquarters in northeast England. Since then, Berghaus has built a reputation for designing gear that withstands the gnarliest conditions. And now the story continues in the United States, where just this year, Berghaus became available to consumers.

Whoever you’re shopping for—friend, family, or yourself—these seven picks from the Berghaus line will upgrade anyone’s holiday season. They’re functional and fashionable and do more than fend off snow, cold, and rain. They make it easier to spend more time outdoors, which is the greatest gift of all.

If your family goes for a holiday hike no matter the weather, this is the jacket for the job. The Kara-Su 7000 GTX is constructed with premium three-layer Gore-Tex Pro, which provides an impenetrable—and superdurable—barrier against snow, sleet, and rain. That superior waterproofing is combined with an adjustable hem and cuffs that further seal out the elements, along with a highly customizable hood.

Six well-placed pockets make it easy to store extras like gloves, candy canes, and other stocking stuffers. The jacket also comes in three bold colorways—ideal for staying seen in a blizzard and always looking good.

Straight-leg pants are having a moment, and the Kara-Su Pant harnesses that style trend and updates it with trail-ready design. Its accommodating cut, articulated knees, and stretch fabric enhance mobility for all outdoor pursuits.

Adjustable ankle hems ensure an always-right fit, and three pockets keep the essentials close at hand. The Kara-Su Pant is built with durable synthetic material that will last for years, which is a good thing because you’ll want to wear them everywhere.

Across the decades, Berghaus apparel has been lauded for matching cutting-edge style with top-notch outdoor performance. For proof, look no further than this retro-chic top: The Kara-Su Sweater turns heads with bright colorblocking, an oversized cut, and the brand’s classic Extrem 7000 logo that ties in Berghaus’s rich history.

Looks aren’t the only appeal, of course. The Kara-Su Sweater’s jersey fabric is snuggle-next-to-the-fire soft, and the ribbed cuffs and hem help it retain its shape over time. Put that all together, and you have a cozy gift that’s guaranteed to be an instant favorite.

This shell blends a sleek, trail-to-town aesthetic with stay-dry-anywhere protection. The Meru Mountain Jacket features two-layer Gore-Tex waterproofing for keeping out every drop of precipitation while remaining breathable enough for hiking, urban exploration, and more. If the weather really starts to kick up, adjustable everything—waist, cuffs, hood, you name it—provides additional protection, complemented by a double storm flap.

Heritage Berghaus branding on the chest and shoulder gives the Meru Mountain Jacket a technical vibe that would look good in any era, and it comes in a variety of eye-catching colorways. Two low-profile zippered pockets near the waist complete the feature set.

This Trango Jacket’s design and performance are just as striking as its looks. Most folks probably notice the latter first: Featuring throwback colors and prominent cargo pockets, the Trango looks like it just stepped out of a time machine from the ’90s. But its performance is all modern: Three-layer Gore-Tex easily sheds moisture, and a hip-length cut provides additional coverage.

Typical of Berghaus jackets, the Trango is packed with smart details: The toggles for adjusting the waist, hood, collar, and hem can be easily operated with gloves on, and the hood’s stiffened brim will keep water from dripping in your eyes. This is the jacket to wear if building a snowman—in a snowstorm—is on the agenda.

For days when you need a little protection and a lot of breathability, the Trango Mountain Windbreaker is the clear choice. It blocks the wind and cuts the chill, and its DWR-treated fabric can withstand light precipitation.

This jacket is wonderfully versatile. The hood offers protection, but on clear days, it stows away to preserve the Trango’s clean lines. The cordlock hem seals out drafts, and two pockets add convenient storage.

When staying warm is priority one, the Trango Mountain Jacket offers a cozy refuge. Synthetic Hydroloft insulation traps heat, and the elasticated, ribbed hem and cuffs ensure warmth stays where it’s needed.

The cropped design works with any outfit, and two large zippered hand pockets can hold a hat, gloves, phone, and more. As always, heritage Berghaus branding on the chest completes the package with a timeless look.

Founded in 1966 in Newcastle upon Tyne, UK, Berghaus is a global leader in premium outdoor outerwear, known for innovative designs and lasting durability. From pioneering lightweight rucksacks to iconic Gore-Tex jackets, Berghaus equips adventurers to tackle the elements.

Michael Carrick has provided an update on Carlos Baleba after the midfielder was forced off during Manchester United’s Champions League win over Roma.

The Cameroon international made a promising run into the Roma box shortly after half-time but then pulled up just as it looked like he would get a shot away.

Baleba immediately grabbed his hamstring and it soon became clear that the medical team would not be able to prolong his evening.

The 22-year-old was in tears as he made his way off the pitch and Carrick said the injury ‘doesn’t look great’ in his post-match press conference after an extraordinary 3-2 comeback victory.

‘I haven’t got any more news on Carlos at this stage but obviously it’s his hamstring and we’ll have to assess it more in the next few days,’ Carrick said.

‘Initially it doesn’t look great but I haven’t got much more to give you than that in terms of a timeframe or anything.’

Following Carrick’s comments, former Manchester United midfielder Nicky Butt said on Amazon Prime: ‘It looks like Baleba will be out for a while now and that won’t help United because they don’t seem to be able to get a settled team out on the pitch.’

LOOK AT THOSE SCENES 🤩From two goals down, Matheus Cunha completes Man Utd's comeback as they defeat Roma 3-2! 👏 pic.twitter.com/EwhKx1rNkY

‘This place does incredible things, magical things and we spoke about it at half-time. I thought we grew into it a little bit first half after giving away the goals too easily.

‘We spoke at half-time about how this place can change, it only takes one moment. The boys made every little bit of every little opportunity really and were fantastic in the end.’

Asked what the remarkable comeback win means for Manchester United, Carrick added: ‘It’s a win for us as a football club.

‘To see the supporters go home happy is what we live for, to give them performances and nights like this so they can get back again energised.

‘As a team, we’ve shown a lot of character to weather what we’ve had to. You find out a lot about each other.

‘The players will find out things about me, I certainly do about them and their character, what it means to them and what they’re prepared to put in.

‘Tonight was right at that moment. It depends what happens next, we’ve got to make the most of it, we’ve got to move forward but I’m feeling a lot better about things tonight.’

Manchester United, now 17th in both the Premier League and Champions League, face Aston Villa at Old Trafford on Sunday.

Devs Are Blaming A Flood Of AI Slop On Steam For Delaying Their Games

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

November is, to put it lightly, nuts for video games. World of Warcraft: Forever launches tomorrow. The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time will be out just a few hours after that. And a short two weeks after that, the juggernaut arrives: GTA 6. So as you might imagine, dozens of indie games are trying to get their releases out now, before these heavy hitters show up and suck all the air out of the room, monopolizing the time of content creators and players who might otherwise pay attention to smaller projects. But the folks behind a lot of those indie games are now worried they might be forced to launch right in the middle of that AAA maelstrom, thanks to what they believe to be a major bottleneck going on internally at Valve; one they think is caused by a flood of AI slop games.

Throughout October, multiple indies have posted on social media suggesting that their approvals process on Steam was taking longer than usual. Normally, Valve says it expects a 3-5 day waiting period when a developer submits a game for approval to appear on the Steam store. Valve then might get back to the developer with any issues it has, and it’s another few days to review again. Once Valve considers any such issues resolved, the game can then be published on the storefront. For all this, Valve specifically advises developers, “you’ll want to submit your page for review at least 7 business days before you want it live to account for potential changes you’ll need to make.”

Except recently, seven business days hasn’t cut it. Throughout October, a number of indie developers have posted on social platforms sharing that their reviews are taking two or three weeks or more to complete for no discernible reason.

There’s Caravarn Carnage: Fruity Road, for instance, which finally made it through Steam approvals on November 1 after submitting on October 16:

Hi @Steamworks, my games build has been stuck in review since October 16, beyond the usual 3-5 business days. I've contacted support but haven't received an update on the build review.

Ashen Legacy submitted on October 15, and was finally approved on November 1, coming in hot ahead of a planned November 6 release date:

Oh my god! My first submission was rejected, and Iâve already had to wait over two weeks. The scariest thing right now isnât the threat of AI-generated game clonesâitâs the wait for Steamâs review process. Itâs a race to see whose game gets approved first, and with my scheduledâ¦ pic.twitter.com/rgbgwK8PVE

— Ashen Legacy | Wishlist on Steam (@VerStudiovn) October 31, 2026

Lacey’s Flash Games was recently forced into a delay; it finally came out on Halloween, four days late:

And Wheel Make It was forced to launch three weeks late:

Wheel Make It has been unintentionally delayed while we're waiting on @Steam's approval.

The game was submitted for review 2 weeks ago, and was rejected because they couldn't figure out how to access local co-op (you literally just share a keyboard…)

The re-review has beenâ¦ pic.twitter.com/jLvB6QHZvK

— 2 Left Thumbs (@2_left_thumbs) October 12, 2026

Here’s a statement from Mira, one of the developers of the similarly-in-limbo Cubic Earth, provided to Kotaku:

We’re a small team, and we’ve spent a long time building up to getting our debut Early Access title, Cubic Earth, out into the world. Having it derailed last minute has been incredibly disheartening. Despite our best effort to follow Valve’s established processes and adherence to their stated timelines, our game now has no known launch window.

We’ve received a number of player complaints and questions across the Steam Community and YouTube, wondering why the game did not launch at the stated time. To make matters worse, organic traffic to our store page from Steam features has dropped off completely, and what was shaping up to be a promising Early Access launch now feels more like an anxiety inducing nightmare.

We’re hoping that this can be resolved as painlessly as possible, and trying to keep our eyes on the future!

Earlier today, I spoke at length to another impacted developer: Alfred Kamon, director of Studio Daimon, which is making Food Devils. Food Devils was supposed to launch today. It won’t be doing that, though, because it’s still awaiting Steam approval after being submitted way back on October 12.

Kamon says he first started to wonder if something was wrong about a week after the submission, with Valve still silent. On October 24, he followed Steam’s published advice for what to do if response was too slow: he submitted a support ticket. On the 26th, Valve responded with a concern about sexual content in the game, to which Kamon immediately replied in an attempt to resolve the issue. Now, seven days after that, he’s still waiting for a reply. Valve also responded to his support ticket, essentially telling him to wait for the review team to get back to him. (Kotaku has seen all of these messages between Kamon and Valve).

Kamon tells me that from his perspective, Studio Daimon did everything right in order to launch on time. Even with Valve having an issue with content, he says that in the past such problems have only taken 2-3 days to resolve, and he had prepared for that. He had not prepared for three weeks of waiting…and counting. He can’t even provide full game keys to reviewers until approvals are done (beta keys can and have gone out).

“Just before the release, everyone is crunching a lot just to finish everything, to do QA, to polish and to prepare all the content,” he said. “So everyone in the team maybe even stayed up late or even during weekends. It was very, very hard. And despite everything, I think it’s sad because everyone made so many sacrifices to be here now today, and we still couldn’t make it.”

What’s really ironic is that Food Devils‘ original release date was actually supposed to be November 5, but 24 hours before the team planned to announce it, Ocarina of Time planted its flag that same day. So the team moved it up. “Oh, no, we are not releasing on the same day as Zelda,” Kamon said. Of course, now there’s a solid chance they might.

I asked Kamon if it made sense to hold the game even longer, maybe into early December. After all, following Ocarina of Time is GTA 6. It’s a terrible month to release a small indie game, and Food Devils was originally getting in just barely under the wire. But Kamon tells me that’s not an option.

Studio Daimon is a small studio: just nine people, based in Italy, working on Food Devils for the last four years. It’s made one other game before, Path of the Midnight Sun, and it’s not exactly rolling in cash. Kamon tells me that the studio really needs the money from sales of Food Devils before the end of the year, and Steam pays out on a one-month delay. If Food Devils can’t launch soon, the studio might be in trouble financially.

Why is all this happening? We reached out to the company for comment and were directed to a blog post from today about the situation. In it, Valve acknowledges that it has seen “a significant increase in submissions for store page and build review in recent months,” noting later that the process is taking “up to 12 business days.” It goes on to say that most of that time is spent re-reviewing games that have issues at submission, and that in addition to adjusting its internal processes to help match the heavier load, Valve is also adding a number of items to its release checklist for developers that it says will help cut down on the number of submissions it needs to send back for adjustments.

What the blog post doesn’t mention is the role of generative AI, which many developers are theorizing is playing an outsized role in the massive wave of submissions. As of mid-October, more games have been released on Steam in 2026 than in all of 2025, with two and a half months to go. And in October alone, around 4,000 games were released on the platform, with nearly 50 percent of them disclosing some use of generative AI in development. That’s a lot of games coming through very, very fast, the speed aided by the ability of generative AI to churn out game-shaped things quickly. Developers suspect that, given these numbers, Valve is struggling to keep up with the sudden influx of submissions, and smaller studios without the clout to ask Valve to expedite their games are suffering for it. But without more specific word from Valve, no one can prove that’s what’s really happening.

The thing is, ValveÂ hasÂ acknowledged that the platform has some issue with AI slop. It finally made a statement last week, after over 8,000 demos flooded Steam Next Fest last month, alongside a report that a developer was extorted by someone threatening to make an AI clone of their project, as well as several developers admitting publicly that they hesitated to release game demos for fear of having their games cloned. But that statement was just that people at Valve didn’t want to be “gatekeepers telling developers which tools they should or shouldn’t use.”

Valve’s Marc Wilkinson also said, “We are fully aware of the situation, and a significant number of people are thinking about how to solve this problem.” But as much as one might appreciate how difficult it may be to figure out how to regulate AI, at the moment, a number of developers are actively suffering with no clear path to relief.

At this point, all Kamon wants is for Food Devils to be approved as soon as possible. He did say it would be nice to get some increased visibility on Steam to make up for this situation, since his release plans have now been all messed up through no fault of his own. And he wishes Valve would be transparent about how long the review process will take.

“I can also sympathize with those who have to review all these games,” he says. “But as a dev, at least I would like some honesty in the UI when you submit a game. Tell us it may take weeks. Just don’t promise us three to five days because then we make plans around the release date. We create hype. We announce everything. People are happy that the game is releasing and in the end, it doesn’t.”

Don Jr.’s Investor Business Partner Abruptly Resigns From Fannie Mae Board

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Conservative businessman Omeed Malik, a close Trump ally and business partner of Donald Trump Jr., has stepped down from the board of Fannie Mae.

The government-sponsored mortgage giant said in a filing with the Securities and Exchange Commission on Tuesday that Malik resigned from the board of directors, effective Oct. 30. Malik is president of the venture capital firm 1789 Capital as well as founder of investment bank Farvahar Partners. Trump Jr. joined 1789 as a partner in November 2024.

Malik "brings extensive experience in financial services and capital markets as well as a blend of legal, entrepreneurial, and strategic leadership expertise," Fannie said in its last annual report in February. He was paid $114,222 last year, that filing shows.

As a member of the eight-member board, Malik has been a part of Fannie Mae as it has enlarged its balance sheet at the behest of President Donald Trump, who pushed the GSE to buy mortgage-backed securities in a bid to lower mortgage rates.

Trump has also floated the idea of ending the conservatorship of Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac, potentially setting the stage for a $30 billion IPO. But the Trump administration has said little of those plans in recent months as Americans grapple with inflation and rising housing costs.

Malik joined the board in April 2025. At the time, Federal Housing Finance Agency Director Bill Pulte, who chairs the board, posted to X that "Omeed brings great capital markets, legal and investment experience as we Make Fannie and Freddie Great Again!"

Fannie Mae reported $4.2 billion in net income in the third quarter. This year it has provided about $344 billion in liquidity to 295,000 homebuyers. The company's overall net worth has swelled from $47.4 billion in 2021 to $119.6 billion in the third quarter.

Tristan Navera is a senior reporter on housing policy, covering trends and solutions in the housing market from Washington, DC. He was previously a senior reporter at Bloomberg Law, and before that covered real estate for the Washington Business Journal. Earlier in his career, he spent a decade reporting on business and real estate in Dayton and Columbus, OH. A Cincinnati native, he holds a journalism degree from Ohio University.

Stablecoin supply falls $17B as Solana card spending hits $141M

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Stablecoin liquidation continues to drop throughout the cryptocurrency markets, reducing capital that will be used to support investor and trader transactions. In April, the total amount of all stable coins was $280 billion, and it has now decreased to $263 billion.

This decrease in value represents a 6% reduction from April to date.

Binance is experiencing a much larger loss than many of the other large cryptocurrency exchanges due to the decrease in its reserve of stablecoins. Prior to the recent losses, Binance held around $51 billion worth of stablecoins in reserve.

Currently, that number stands at approximately $33.9 billion. The recent losses experienced by Binance are reflective of the recent hacking incident, which saw an approximate loss of $3 billion removed from the platform.

As investors withdraw their stablecoins and transfer their assets to other cryptocurrency platforms, they are taking away purchasing power from these exchanges.

When exchanges lose purchasing power, the demand for products purchased using cryptocurrencies can suffer. This could result in increased downward pressure on prices.

A similar effect is being felt on Ethereum’s [ETH] ability to rapidly purchase any asset based on immediate needs for such. Over the past thirty days, Binance’s Ethereum-based reserves for Tether [USDT] have been reduced by 7.8% to $34.78 billion.

The declines in reserves reflect ongoing withdrawals. For example, each day for the past month has reflected an average weekly net flow of withdrawals that has resulted in a negative balance.

Although some funds may have moved into self-custody or other platforms, Binance now holds less capital ready for trading.

This weakness coincides with muted ETH spot volumes, limited exchange accumulation, and continued outflows from Ethereum ETFs. As both institutional and spot demand decrease, so do the availability of buyers willing to acquire selling assets.

Therefore, ETH price action is facing increasing downward pressures until renewed influxes of USDT occur into exchanges and demand for investments increases.

While exchange liquidity weakens, Solana [SOL] is recording increases in payments made utilizing stablecoins. On the 31st of October, the total monthly volume of card spending reached a record high of $141 million.

This was a sevenfold increase compared to October 2025 and represented a significant increase since spending first crossed the $50 million mark per month in January 2026.

The recent increase indicates an increasing reliance upon Solana-based stablecoins for daily use cases. Therefore, regardless of declining global cryptocurrency liquidations, Solana is gaining an additional form of network demand through utility.

The total stablecoin market capitalization fell 6% to $263 billion, while Binance’s Tether [USDT] reserves dropped 7.8% in 30 days.

Solana [SOL] stablecoin card spending reached a record $141 million in October, marking sevenfold year-on-year growth.

The Wendy’s JRPG Is a Searing Anti-Capitalist Screed About Humans Making Real Art

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

'Wendy's should not have a game that's this good'

The resistance against the AI art apocalypse is being televised in a marketing campaign about burgers.

Over the past year, Wendy’s — yes, the same Wendy’s known for its square burgers, thick milkshakes, and unlikely stewardship of a mid-tier donut chain — has made waves through a series of unconventional creative partnerships. Amid slumps in sales and attention, Wendy’s has turned to popular artistic mediums, like music and video games, to try and reverse course.

The buzziest Wendy’s-affiliated project was the post-hardcore EP Songs to Listen to in a Wendy’s Parking Lot, which featured six tracks written by musicians from bands with legit emo cred, like Anberlin, Blessthefall, Silverstein, and Winona Fighter. Look, I am as sorry as you are to hand anything to Big Corporate, but all six tracks are straight bangers. (Just listen to “She Said She Didn’t Want Fries” and try to tell me you didn’t have that on repeat in a Wendy’s parking lot in 2008.) On the heels of that album release is the classic JRPG riff WendyQuest: Legend of the Four Corner Stones. Like Songs to Listen to, it boasts a similar unmistakable authenticity.

“Wendy’s should not have a game that’s this good,” Stephane Cotichini, founder and game director at 81Monkeys, the studio behind WendyQuest, told Polygon in a video call. (For the record, 81Monkeys was not named after the viral 2025 news story about meth dealers attempting to smuggle 81 monkeys across the Thailand-Cambodia border. Pure coincidence. “Somebody forwarded me the article the day it broke and I was, like, ‘Seriously? How?!’” Cotichini said. “Of all the numbers of monkeys, it had to be our number?”)

WendyQuest started as many projects in the indie gaming world do: funding. Or, rather, a lack thereof.

For the past several years, 81Monkeys has been working on an ambitious, systems-heavy pixel art RPG called World of Anterra. Though World of Anterra was successfully funded through Kickstarter in 2022, the scope and ambition of the game continued to swell, and the team found itself rapidly running out of runway. But as any indie or even mid-sized game developer will tell you, funding in the games industry has all but evaporated since the early 2020s gold rush. Things were looking “dire,” according to Cotichini, who said he had post-Kickstarter funding conversations with 60 prospects that went nowhere. Then an email landed in his inbox through the studio’s website form.

“Normally, I would have ignored the email, but because he said they're fans of the game, I was like, ‘Oh, I'll hear them out.’ So I get on a call and I find out it was Wendy's. And when I found out what the game was, I instantly knew we were the right team,” Cotichini said.

Within a week, 81Monkeys signed the deal for WendyQuest, allowing the studio to continue working on World of Anterra. 81Monkeys split its team 50/50 to work on both games.

The Wendy’s people apparently had the general picture and the overarching creative vision for WendyQuest, but the team at 81Monkeys still had its work cut out for it in turning it into a viable video game. They revised the battle system away from purely turn-based combat to a more active one. This echoes one of the studio’s core principles: Nostalgia has to feel contemporary, otherwise it’s just going to feel dated. Cotichini also acknowledged that while the original script had strong ideas (the Wendy’s marketing team is known for its witty social media posts), it didn’t quite click for a video game, where the writing isn’t just about telling a narrative but also about making a world feel real and lived-in.

Ultimately, writers at 81Monkeys rewrote “99%” of the script, according to Cotichini. Everything still had to fly by corporate for brand-safety reasons, but there are some unmistakable nods to the competition: The logo for WendyQuest’s villain, Dr. Mechborg, is a giant golden “M.” “Sure, sure, serving billions is great,” MechBorg says in his introductory villain proselytizing that’s mandatory for most every JRPG. “But it’s time I make billions.” He then directs his army to “cut corners.”

I’ve played a little bit of WendyQuest, and to quote the kids, it slaps. Beyond its immediate 16-bit-era vibes, the combat system is the main draw. The system is built around a gauge that rapidly fills up at the bottom of the screen. You have to press the attack button as close to full as possible; tap too early and you’ll deal less damage or miss your attack, but if you get it right, you’ll deal maximum damage. It’s a nice touch that keeps WendyQuest from feeling like yet another Final Fantasy clone. The game is pretty funny, too; its jokes-per-minute ratio is on par with TV shows like Veep or 30 Rock. Cotichini is right: Wendy’s should not have a game that’s this good.

“Initially, they weren't gonna credit us,” Cotichini said. “I mean, that's the nature of this type of agreement, right? We're just a silent partner, we'll make this game, and then they launch it.”

But he pushed back. In late 2023, when the project initially began development, AI was out in the wild, though not yet the industry-demolishing force it is today. AI grows more unpopular every year, but gaming audiences in particular react with visceral repudiation; even a trailer that features AI-generated assets can prompt a major company to reverse on a dime and issue a public apology.

“You want people to know that it was 100% human-made by an actual indie studio,” Cotichini said.

That ethos is reflected in a line of bolded text front and center on the Steam pages for both WendyQuest and World of Anterra: “100% human made.”

“Part of why World of Anterra is as good as it is, and I know the world hasn't played it yet, neither have you, but it's because of the time it took to bring it to life. It's because of the struggle it took. All of that comes across in the product that ends up being created, and I think that's essential. So the more AI is used to make a game, the worse the game becomes,” Cotichini said. “Because the more you speed up that process, the less opportunity you have to improve it.”

Earlier in World of Anterra’s development, 81Monkeys dabbled with AI for project management and efficiency, but found that despite promises peddled by the likes of Sam Altman and Dario Amodei, the tools actually increased costs and decreased productivity. They also found that generative AI offered solutions for problems that already had solutions. Cotichini floated the idea of using AI to help speed up the creation of NPCs, for instance. “They were, like, ‘Why don’t we just make a script to do that?’”

For WendyQuest, according to Cotichini, the marketing team at Wendy’s actively pushed 81Monkeys to steer clear of AI tools and workflows.

No one could claim that WendyQuest is as in-depth as, say, Final Fantasy Resonance or other big-budget RPGs capitalizing on the nostalgia trend du jour. But there’s a real richness — and more importantly, a real authenticity — to its design. This is why Songs to Listen to in a Wendy’s Parking Lot ended up resonating so much with retired scene kids. These were real songs written by legit artists who we actually listened to in Wendy’s parking lots 20 years ago. In an era where McDonald’s is using AI to create nightmare fever dream ads and implement dynamic pricing algorithms, Wendy’s investing instead in the arts stands out.

“I think that creative work in general has gotten watered down over the last couple decades,” Cotichini said. “And the reason is because it became more influenced and controlled by business interest and less by the creative. Now, I actually see a swing the other way.”

A weekly cheat sheet to what you’ll eventually love to play. No buying, no commitment — just saving for later.

That might sound like an oxymoron coming from the mouth of the guy who’s making a game for a company with a $2.2 billion market cap. But really, why should it? Is the Wendy’s logo being on this thing any different than Sony’s or Microsoft’s or, hell, even Nintendo’s? (None of gaming’s three biggest companies started out as video game publishers.) Yes, it’s marketing, and yes, it is transparently transactional. But the games industry is facing down its fifth year of a dry spell for traditional funding models. That’s not changing any time soon. If a Fortune 500 company was debating how to allocate 0.5% of its 2027 marketing budget, would you rather it be spent on a month of YouTube preroll or on a year of overhead for a budding independent studio?

“The industry's really suffering right now. Things have never looked more dire,” Cotichini said. “This is the thing that I hope will come of WendyQuest. Wendy’s spends some millions of dollars in marketing every year. WendyQuest costs a fraction of that. So many brands out there do the same thing. My hope is that when people see WendyQuest — and that, actually, if a studio cares about a branded game, it can be a really good game — that more brands seek partnerships like we made with Wendy's. I think that could become an alternate funding source for so many really talented indies in the industry.”

Bruce Straley isn't all too fond of generative AI art

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

This is giving me flashbacks to the time Wendys released a custom TTRPG, paid Critical Role to play a one-shot in their system, and the backlash from fans was so strong Critical Role never uploaded the VOD and donated all the proceeds they earned from that stream to a charity that supports farmworkers' rights.

And it was a great system, nonetheless. Shame that people have to be so annoying.

props to the artists who did this work but corporate propaganda no thanks.

A lot of these stylish, boutique hotels enter the points world through curated hotel collections that participate in loyalty programs, giving hotels a wider reach of potential guests without having to adhere to strict brand standards that change the spirit of the property.

One of the pioneers in this space is Design Hotels, a collection of hundreds of independently-owned hotels and resorts dotting the globe, each with its own identity, personality and approach to hospitality.

"Every hotel is picked for its originality [and] the vision that's behind it," Stijn Oyen, managing director at Design Hotels, told TPG, adding that each property has "a strong point of view and cultural connection."

And it's not necessarily easy to make the cut for this collection: According to Oyen, only about 5% of the hotels that apply to join Design Hotels are accepted in an effort to "stay true to the original roots and values of what makes a design hotel."

Then there's the loyalty twist: Marriott actually owns Design Hotels, and many (but not all) of the collection's properties participate in Marriott Bonvoy.

Here's a quick guide to everything you need to know about Design Hotels, plus a few standout properties where you can earn and burn Marriott Bonvoy points.

Related: 12 new boutique hotels worth redeeming your points for

Founded in the early 1990s, Design Hotels was created to bring together independently owned, design-forward hotels that shared a commitment to originality, creativity and local culture.

Today, that collection is more than 300 members strong, with new properties regularly joining. What differentiates the collection is the individuality of each property — each one has its own unique point of view, rather than a prescribed set of standards travelers will see at more traditional hotel brands.

"We're set up around design," Oyen explained. "It's about the vision. It's about being independent with a voice."

Design Hotels became part of Marriott's portfolio following the hotel giant's acquisition of Starwood Hotels & Resorts in 2016, giving Marriott a majority stake in the company. By 2021, Marriott had acquired full ownership of Design Hotels, though the collection continues to operate as a separate entity, with member hotels retaining their individual identities and independence.

The company describes its properties as "design-driven hotels that function as social hubs and spaces for purposeful experiences."

But what does that actually mean? To start, getting into the collection isn't easy, and Design Hotels is looking for one distinguishing factor before choosing a new member: "the originality in its concept."

According to Oyen, only about 5% of applicants pass the test. Hotels are also evaluated on everything from design and sustainability to cultural programming and the property's connection to its local community.

"It's a strong, strong sense of place," Oyen explained, adding that beyond their physical design, the hotels should have vibrant public spaces that attract locals and feel like an extension of the neighborhoods they call home.

It's also about the people. According to Oyen, about 60% of Design Hotels owners are first-time hoteliers, bringing fresh perspectives and new approaches to hospitality. The company calls the creative minds behind its properties "Originals," describing them as "the engines behind pioneering design and revolutionary hospitality," according to the brand's website.

Without rigid brand standards dictating every detail, these hoteliers have the freedom to pursue projects that seasoned hospitality pros might consider too risky. That might be as simple as removing TVs from guest rooms or as dramatic as building a hotel accessible only by cable car, like Vigilius Mountain Resort in Italy's Dolomites.

For Marriott Bonvoy members, Design Hotels offers a truly independent, boutique-like stay while still allowing them to earn and burn points. Something that, frankly, a lot of people don't realize is available to them in the loyalty world.

Just don't forget: Not all Design Hotels participate in Marriott Bonvoy. To see a full list of the 120-plus participating hotels, visit Marriott's dedicated Design Hotels landing page.

For Design Hotels, according to Oyen, the relationship with Marriott is a win-win situation: Hotels gain access to nearly 300 million loyalty members while maintaining their "complete independence."

But because these properties operate differently from traditional Marriott hotels, Bonvoy elite members shouldn't expect all their usual perks to apply.

Here's what members can expect based on their status.

The following Marriott Bonvoy benefits are excluded at Design Hotels:

Set near the beach in Casablanca, Villa Sahrai pays respect to "Casablanca’s Art Deco tradition" across its 56 rooms and public spaces. Be sure to take advantage of the spa and its Moroccan hammam before settling down for food at one of two restaurants or a nightcap at the bar.

How to book: Cash rates from around $160 per night or 34,000 Marriott Bonvoy points per night.

To get to Vigilius Mountain Resort, guests must ascend a private cable car that travels between the mountain hotel and the village it soars above. But once up at the top, guests are treated to beautiful Dolomite views, architecture that incorporates nature and wellness experiences around every stylish corner.

How to book: Cash rates from about $341 per night or 47,000 Marriott Bonvoy points.

At The Austin Proper Hotel, patterns, textures and colors combine in unexpected and beautiful ways from the moment guests step inside until their head hits the pillow. The hotel is as cool as Austin itself, but also full of surprises: You can grab classic Tex-Mex to eat or opt for a French Japanese tasting menu or soak up the sun at the rooftop pool before a lymphatic drain at the Verbena Spa.

How to book: Cash rates from $350 or 54,000 Marriott Bonvoy points.

Kyoto is no stranger to incredible hotels, but Genji Kyoto offers an intimate experience with just 20 rooms that feel distinctly Japanese and totally sleek and fresh. As serene as they come, spend a day getting lost in Kyoto's ancient history before heading to the rooftop bar and garden to unwind.

How to book: Cash rates from about $227 per night or 47,000 Marriott Bonvoy points.

Condesa is one of Mexico City's trendiest neighborhoods, so it only makes sense to stay in a hotel that also fits the description. At this hotel, enjoy rooftop sushi or French-Mexican fusion food; relax in the hydrotherapy pool or kick back on the patio and watch the city's cosmopolitan set roam the area.

How to book: Cash rates from $339 per night or 42,000 Marriott Bonvoy points.

As major hotel companies continue to expand their portfolios beyond traditional hotels, from independent boutique properties to outdoor-focused experiences, collections like Design Hotels are helping diversify what travelers can experience with points and miles.

For Marriott Bonvoy members, that means more opportunities to stay somewhere truly unique without giving up the ability to earn and redeem points. And while the elite benefits might look a little different, the chance to experience a hotel with its own distinct personality might just be worth the trade-off.

The Mad (Color) Science Behind Rian Johnson’s New Camera App

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

You shouldn’t ever take a photo of your midterm election ballot — it’s illegal in some states — but, thanks to Rian Johnson and his longtime DP Steve Yedlin, there is now a very cinematic way to take iPhone photos of everything else.

Yedlin has spent more than 20 years as a cinematographer, collaborating closely with Johnson on everything from “Brick” and “The Brothers Bloom” to “The Last Jedi” and the “Knives Out” films. But Yedlin has also spent 20 years delving into the science of color and how filmmakers can better craft imagery through the technical tools available. And now he and his friends are in the business of making new ones. Related Stories 5 Big Challenges in Creating Criterion’s ‘The Complete Kubrick’: From Navigating HDR/4K to Preserving Stanley Kubrick’s Love of Mono Sound Ebenezer’s Venmo and ‘How to Rob a Bank’ ATMs: Studios Are Now Promoting Movies by Just Giving Away Money

Yedlin, Johnson, and color science coder Eric Cameron have been in the process of creating new software for light controls to replace traditional dimmer boards, pixel mapping, and on-screen playback (ie., when actors are looking at phone or computer screens on camera) with something that embraces the world of color-tunable lights and LEDs. But as a side project, they’ve also just launched Chromagraph, an iPhone app that uses Yedlin’s deep, mathematical understanding of color to bypass the iPhone’s default photo processing settings for a more filmic look.

The app project started because Johnson wanted something on his phone that could capture photos the way that Yedlin captures film — using RAW images (ie., everything the sensor captures, not just what’s rendered on a screen) and bypassing the standard iPhone image processing protocols.

Surprisingly, it only took ten weeks to build, test, and refine the app Johnson wanted to a place the team was happy with it — although Yedlin admittedly told IndieWire the process to create Chromagraph was still pretty intense. The cinematographer had done the math to build an image processing look that works for cinema cameras like the Arri ALEXA, but even the newer, pro-level iPhone cameras have sensors so small they’re smol, and they also spread image information out in a different way from how film cameras work.

“ The code values themselves, like the actual numbers in the pixels, alone don’t tell you how bright something’s supposed to be. It’s the code values in the pixels plus this [metadata] evaluation,” Yedlin said of iPhone cameras, before worrying he was being way too technical for a 900-word article. “It’s not like we’re putting different parameters into existing processing math. It’s totally different math for the most part.”

To make a complicated story shorter, then, Yedlin and Cameron couldn’t reprogram the iPhone camera, but they could get it to behave differently in order to bring RAW images (everything the camera sensor captures) into Chromagraph and then do a bunch of different and complicated math to achieve the image processing for the cinematic look that Rian Johnson wanted to be able to pull up at the swipe of a screen.

The main thing that was important to the team to get right and reflect real color science, as opposed to “bullshit cinema color science,” according to Yedlin, was to work from the truth that images that look aesthetically pleasing are not clinical or technical reproductions. They are not photometrically or colorimetrically “correct.”

“What makes a good image is something that looks enough like what it’s representing to be recognizable and then to be beautiful,” Yedlin said. “Even when you have a very clinical-looking, like a broadcast look as opposed to a cinema look, it’s also not photometrically or colorimetrically correct. They’re just not correct in a different way, you know? It’s always a matter of taste.”

A lot of the very complicated math that went into how Chromagraph does image processing is based on Yedlin and Johnson’s taste for how to make an image look artful, but then the app has contrast and saturation tools for individual photographers to take the look of their work even further. The tools inside of Chromagraph are, in fact, meant to be exactly like the math of white balance in ISO on a film camera.

“It sort of gets repeated as giving photos the ‘Knives Out’ look, and that’s fine if people want to say that, but I don’t think of it that way. I think of it as generally artful rendering. Every single movie made with traditional film stocks was made with one of like three negatives and one of two print stocks, and they all have completely different looks based on the craftsmanship and the photography and what’s being photographed and lighting and everything,” Yedlin said. “This is just the image processing, so it’s one look for that part, but the image processing part isn’t the only thing that makes a photo.”

Where Chromagraph can really help photographers make their own photos, though, is in capturing color in a way that the iPhone default method can’t quite reach. “Vibrant colors is a place where I think Chromagraph really shows off. A lot of times, with garish colors, you can have that saturation clamped look where one of the three channels is slammed so the entire area kinda looks like it’s been just colored in like a coloring book,” Yedlin said. “So some people are posting photos [from the app] where the colors really stand out, but then somebody else is posting photos with the most subtle, nuanced colors. It’s only one look, but it’s for that last little bit of finesse for whatever kind of photo you take.”

For that reason, Yedlin, Johnson, and Cameron don’t necessarily want to support multiple looks inside of Chromagraph. They’re also not planning on building an Android version anytime soon, and while they’d like to support video, that’s still a long way from being worked out. But for photographers with an iPhone in their pocket, the app is a fun, easy shortcut past importing photos into professional post tools.

“It’s satisfying that every snapshot looks the way that we like it,” Yedlin said.

Chromagraph is now available in the Apple App Store.

Is Your Dog Weird Enough for Times Square?

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

We just sent a welcome email your way with a quick overview of what to expect (including those vital recall alerts).

Dogster is reader-supported. When you buy via links on our site, we may earn a commission at no cost to you. Read about privacy.

Does your pup have a bedtime routine that makes no sense, a suspicious obsession with household objects, or dramatically howl at their own reflection in the oven door? Congratulations. You might be living with a contender for the World's Weirdest Dog. Embark Veterinary has officially launched its search for canine oddballs, inviting pet parents to submit photos and videos of their furballâs most gloriously bizarre behaviors before November 15, 2026. The winning pup will earn the ultimate claim to canine fame: their face on a Times Square billboard, the official title of World's Weirdest Dog, and a $200 gift card.

The contest celebrates the behaviors that make dogs funny, unique, and truly memorable; the ones that make us smile when we think about them, while also highlighting  Embark's Behavior Insights & Training feature, which explores how canine DNA may influence behavior.

Sock Thieves, Bathtub Sleepers, and Other Household Oddballs

Furry weirdos have interesting, different habits; Embark's examples include a dog who sleeps upside down in the bathtub, one who carries a sock around without chewing it, and a canine who spins exactly three times before lying down. Not two. Not four. Three.

Then there's the dog who refuses to walk past one particular house, or the dinner diva who won't touch a meal until someone has given it the full restaurant treatment.

Such behaviors are entertaining partly because they are so unique and specific. Most pet parents can share at least one story that would make a perfectly reasonable person wonder what goes on inside that furry little head.

A sock might be a very treasured object, a source of comfort, a reminder of their favorite personâs scent, or simply something fun to carry. A particular walking route might feel uncomfortable for reasons a person cannot detect, most likely a scentâ¦ but then again, who knows? And a bedtime ritual may have started with nothing more mysterious than finding a comfortable position.

Of course, some dogs are just little weirdos, and that's part of their charm. But there's a difference between a pup who has always slept with their legs in the air and one who suddenly starts acting strangely. If your pup begins staring at walls, repeating the same movements, or turning down food when that's not normal for them, don't automatically label it as a new amusing personality trait. Pain, illness, and anxiety can all show up as changes in behavior. If something feels off, put the camera down and call the vet.

Genes can influence behavioral tendencies, but they don't write a complete script for an individual dog's life. Learning and environment play a big role, too, sometimes in ways pet parents never intended.

Take the sock thief. A dog grabs a sock, their pet parents react with dramatic excitement, and suddenly the household is involved in a thrilling chase. From our perspective, it's a laundry crime. From the pup's perspective, it may be an excellent new game.

Behaviors that lead to positive or rewarding outcomes are more likely to happen again. Attention, play, food, and access to preferred activities can all reinforce behavior, even when nobody is training anything on purpose.

Genetics can contribute, too. Inherited tendencies may influence a dog's motivation to chase, retrieve, explore, or seek food. But a predisposition doesn't guarantee a particular behavior, nor does it explain every individual habit. A dog's behavior comes from a combination of biology and learned experience, which is often less convenient than blaming everything on their breed, but it is much closer to reality.

A 2022 study published in Science looked at the behavior and DNA of thousands of dogs. Researchers found that a dog's breed explained only about 9% of the differences in behavior between individual dogs. In other words, dogs of the same breed can have very different personalities and behaviors. Genetics plays a role, but it doesn't tell the whole story.

That doesn't mean genetics is irrelevant. The researchers found evidence that many behavioral traits do have a heritable component. Rather, the findings show why breed alone is a poor crystal ball for predicting how an individual dog will behave.

Some dogs may be especially motivated to retrieve, chase, or investigate, reflecting tendencies shaped partly by their ancestry and breeding history. But individual dogs still vary, and experience helps shape how those tendencies appear. However, with more data, we may learn new things; science is ever evolving along with our technologies.

Embark's Behavior Insights & Training feature connects genetic information with behavioral insights and training guidance. Genetic results can be useful, but they are one piece of the puzzle and not a personality prediction.

Behavior is best understood in context. Has the habit been present for years, or did it start last week? What happens before the behavior? Could pain, fear, stress, or another medical issue be contributing?

If a pup is safe and comfortable, there's often no reason to stop a peculiar routine unless itâs a problem. A dog who carries a favorite toy around the house doesn't need a corrective training plan. When a behavior does create a problem, redirection and positive reinforcement can help teach an alternative response without relying on intimidation or punishment. This is the science of behavioral modification.

The goal isn't to turn every pup into a perfectly polished obedience champion, but to help us understand what each dog needs and help them become great family members.

Pet parents can enroll their weirdos through November 15, 2026, by uploading a photo or video of their pup's strangest behavior, with an optional short story, at Embark's official contest page. No weird dog? No problem. You can still get involved by casting your vote for the weirdest dog through the official voting page.

The first-place winner will receive the World's Weirdest Dog title, a Times Square billboard feature, and a $200 gift card. Second through fifth place will receive $150 gift cards each, while sixth through fifteenth place will receive an Embark Breed + Health Dog DNA Test with the Behavior Insights & Training upgrade.

So, if you know your furry pal is a weirdo, this may be their moment. Let that freak flag fly!

Canva is announcing changes and layoffs affecting 22 percent of its staff

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Canva is making significant changes affecting 1,271 people, which is 22 percent of its staff, co-founder and CEO Melanie Perkins announced today. “For some people, this will mean changes to their roles or being asked to relocate to centralised hubs,” says Perkins. “Sadly, it will also mean saying goodbye to teammates and friends who have helped build Canva into what it is today.”

After exploring a wide range of alternatives, I’ve come to the difficult conclusion that small changes won’t be enough. To set ourselves up for the next decade, we need to refound Canva, getting back to our roots of small, empowered teams with the freedom to make decisions, take ownership and focus on what matters most for our community.

Canva’s changes include bringing its Product, Growth, and Platform teams together into one product organization, “simplifying layers” and “consolidating overlapping responsibilities,” and making Sydney the “primary home for more of our product teams, as well as roles in other Specialties where shared context and close collaboration are especially important.” Starting in January, Canva will also “expect” most hybrid employees to spend 2 to 3 days in an office each week “depending on what makes sense in your area.”

Employees leaving Canva will get benefits including at least six months of salary, twelve months of healthcare coverage, and career support resources.

Yesterday, Squarespace announced layoffs affecting 19 percent of its staff.

“Love to the Point of Invention” – Injured Kitten with a Tiny Toy-Car Wheelchair Finds His Forever Home

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Catster is reader-supported. When you buy via links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission at no cost to you. Learn more.

Some kittens steal your heart with their big eyes. Others win you over with their playful antics. And then there's Gelato, a tiny kitten who needed a wheelchair and ended up getting one made from Hot Wheels cars. Yes, really. Human ingenuity and humane creativity to the rescue!

Becoming more mobile in a Hot Wheels wheelchair might seem like a dream come true, but today, Gelato has even more reasons to celebrate: Heâs found his forever home.

Gelato arrived at Cincinnati Animal CARE in Ohio as a stray kitten with a lower-back injury that left him unable to use his hind legs normally. The shelter team wanted to help him move around, but there was a rather inconvenient problem. The wheelchairs they had available were too big for his tiny body.

Now, anyone who has lived with a kitten knows that, besides being irresistibly cute, they can be remarkably determined little creatures. They want to explore everything, investigate every corner, and get involved in whatever their humans are doing. Being unable to move around easily can limit those everyday experiences for the tiny fur balls.

Fortunately, Gelato had people in his corner who weren't about to let his small size stop him from exploring the big world.

The shelter's lead veterinary technician, Mallory Smith, came up with a wonderfully resourceful solution. She built a miniature wheelchair using cardboard, tape, zip ties, wooden rulers, and Hot Wheels toy cars. The first version needed adjustments, but Mallory kept working until the little device was ready to support Gelato as he moved around. While this is certainly quite a unique solution, what isnât unusual is the compassion and creativity behind it. Gelatoâs improvised mobility device is a perfect example of how animal medical care personnel go above and beyond to help patients in need.

Gelato's homemade wheelchair was a clever solution to an immediate problem. However, a makeshift device is not necessarily the best long-term option for a growing kitten with mobility challenges.

Help arrived in the form of an engineer who created a custom 3D-printed wheelchair designed to fit his body and accommodate his growth. The toy cars were gone, but Gelatoâs future mobility was looking more stable.

Gelato got lucky the moment someone refused to give up and asked, "How can we make this work?" This first lucky chance opened him to new opportunities from a wider effort to help one small animal have a better chance at life. Itâs impressive how sometimes sharing an animal's story can make all the difference. A social media post can connect shelters with people who have the skills, resources, or homes that animals need.

Giving Gelato a way to move around was an important first step. And there was another milestone waiting for him: a loving family of his own.

As the story of Gelatoâs unusual mode of transport spread, drawing plenty of social media attention, it also caught the eye of Hope Hollandsworth, a 28-year-old tattoo artist and devoted cat parent. When she first saw a photograph of Gelato during the summer, she felt immediately drawn to him.

Hollandsworth already cared for two cats with spinal injuries, Bread Crumb and Sprinkles. Both have limited use of their hind legs and rely on their front legs for mobility. She understood that caring for cats with special needs could involve extra responsibilities, adjustments, and plenty of patience.

In other words, she wasn't going into this adoption without knowing that life with a disabled cat can look a little different. Cat lovers are abundant, but those experienced in caring for disabled felines? Not quite as plentiful.

Hope wasted no time, adopting Gelato as soon as she met him at the shelter. The five-month-old kitten spent his first night in his new home sleeping in her arms.

Absolutely. A disability does not automatically mean that a cat has a poor quality of life. Cats with mobility challenges can still enjoy affection, play, exploration, food puzzles, comfortable naps, and the company of people they trust.

What really matters is understanding the individual cat's needs and making reasonable adjustments to help them live comfortably.

For a cat with a spinal injury, this may include providing easy access to food, water, resting areas, and litter boxes. Non-slip flooring, ramps, and thoughtfully arranged furniture can also help some cats move around more safely. Depending on the injury, a cat may need help with bladder or bowel function, regular skin checks, or a rehabilitation plan.

These are not small details. Some cats with spinal injuries cannot empty their bladders normally, which can lead to serious complications without very devoted care. A veterinarian can assess bladder function, mobility, sensation, and other concerns, then explain what support the cat will need at home. The real challenge is finding someone with the time and patience to take over these responsibilities on a daily basis.

Hollandsworth's experience with Bread Crumb and Sprinkles gave her a head start.

Gelato will continue receiving therapy in the hope that he may regain some use of his hind legs, but for now, his recovery is uncertain. Spinal injuries vary, and a cat's prognosis depends on several factors, including the extent of the damage and the neurological function that remains.

But Gelato doesn't need a guaranteed recovery to deserve a happy future. He's got somewhere to go, someone to cuddle, and absolutely no need to prove that he can walk to earn his place in the family.

His new family is ready to love and support him, whether or not he ever walks on his own. Hollandsworth put it beautifully: to her, Gelato is already perfect just the way he is.

There is a lot of wisdom in that simple sentiment. We can hope for progress, celebrate small improvements, and pursue appropriate treatment without making an animal's worth depend on how much they recover. Loving others, whether animals, people, or even ourselves, for who they are, exactly as they are, is what we call true love.

Dr. Paola Cuevas is the Senior In-House Veterinarian at Pangolia, the parent company of Catster. She has over 18 years of experience as a veterinarian and animal behaviorist. Paola's focus is on preventive medicine and animal welfare. Paola has a large range of skills in from diagnostic imaging to nutrition and clinical pathology. Paola enjoys sharing her extensive knowledge of animal training with our Catster readers and is always happy to answer reader questions as our in-house veterinarian.

Bruno Fernandes confession: ‘I was lucky on first penalty, then made a decision’

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Bruno Fernandes explained what went through his mind when retaking that penalty to revive Manchester United with Roma. ‘I was lucky honestly, but the second time I made a decision.’

The Giallorossi had been dominating at Old Trafford in the Champions League this evening, leading 2-0 after 18 minutes with a Matias Soulé strike and Donyell Malen spot-kick.

Manchester United were given the opportunity to get back into it with a soft penalty, but Bruno Fernandes saw his effort parried by Devis Vasquez.

As Bryan Cristante encroached to make the clearance on the rebound, VAR ruled it had to be retaken, and this time Bruno Fernandes converted to start the comeback, eventually winning 3-2.

“I knew it was a big responsibility, it could’ve changed the game and we really needed a goal to get the crowd behind us at that moment,” Bruno Fernandes told Sky Sport Italia.

“The penalty is always a stressful moment, because it is just you against the goalkeeper and nobody else can help in that situation.

“I was lucky honestly that Cristante went into the box too early, so it gave me an opportunity to take it again. The second time, I decided to pick a spot and hit it as hard as I could without trying to second-guess where the goalkeeper was going.”

After the 4-1 defeat to Como in the Champions League, it was reported the Manchester United staff refused to let Bruno Fernandes speak to the local media in Italian, even though he spent many years in Serie A and B.

Evidently something changed, because the Portugal international was able to converse in Italian this evening and discussed the current crisis at Manchester United, who had been without a win since September 10.

“It is a very important win, because obviously things aren’t going the way we want them to. We aim to do one thing and we do another, we concede goals at the start of games, it is a truly difficult moment, but we must try to get ourselves out of it,” added the midfielder.

“I hope this can give us the confidence to go forward and do better, because there is quality in this squad, we saw what we did last season and it’s not possible that it all just disappeared.

“These are the same players as before, we have added some good quality over the summer, and we need to keep going.”

Mamdani and others have called out Google's decision to disable search functionality for schools who opt out of its AI tools.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Saying that it believes "schools should have control over AI in their classrooms," Google announced today that it will turn off access to its AI tools for K-12 student accounts. But there's a catch: Along with those AI tools, it will also disable Google search functionality.

In a blog post entitled "Designing responsible AI for learning," Chris Phillips, Google's vice president and general manager of education, extolled the virtues of AI tools in "productive struggle," which he described as "enabling students to be actively challenged without getting so overwhelmed that they give up."

"That's the philosophy behind Guided Learning," Philips wrote. "In Gemini, Guided Learning acts as an interactive study tool that coaches students through the process of problem solving through open-ended questions and step-by-step breakdowns. Our research shows that Guided Learning leads to measurable learning gains for students, and we're now bringing Guided Learning to more products." Those products include Google Search and Google Classroom.

"After listening to schools, teachers, leading researchers, and policymakers, we are now updating our approach to controls for Gemini, Gemini Notebook, Gemini in Classroom, and Google Search," Phillips continued. "Access to these products will be entirely off for K-12 student accounts until school administrators explicitly turn them on."

It sounds like Google Search on student accounts can be re-enabled separately, but that also ushers AI back into the equation: Google said in the update that it is "building Guided Learning capabilities into Search for student accounts to help them work through problems without skipping straight to answers." In other words, there is no search functionality on these accounts without AI intrusion.

Speaking to the New York Times, Phillips said "search and AI are deeply integrated," and confirmed that schools can't have one without the other: Students at schools that have turned off Google's AI tools "will not have access to search, and they'll see a message from their school that indicates it's not available."

Phillips presents the policy as responsiveness and responsibility on Google's part, but the reaction from educators is not entirely positive. American Federation of Teachers president Randi Weingarten told NBC that "Google has prioritized its business interests over the education of our children," adding, "Instead of working with teachers and parents to uphold privacy and ensure their tools are safe and useful, it throws the baby out with the bathwater and bans access to the tools altogether."

Keep up to date with the most important stories and the best deals, as picked by the PC Gamer team.

Fairplay, a non-profit that aims to protect children from "the exploitative and harmful business practices of marketers and Big Tech," was somewhat harsher in its assessment. "Google's attempt to strong-arm schools into using its AI products is likely to backfire," Fairplay executive director Josh Golin said in a statement. "Schools should use this opportunity not only to turn off AI, but to teach students that 'Google' isn't a verb, but the name of a surveillance and advertising company whose presence in schools has nothing to do with learning and everything to do with developing lifetime brand loyalty.

"Because AI isn't safe for kids and undermines education and critical thinking, schools should turn off Google's AI tools the minute that option becomes available. And rather than fret about losing access to Google search, schools should use this opportunity to help students find more privacy-protective alternatives for search, as well as ways to do more substantive research beyond search and click."

Former FTC chair Lina Khan, who was right about Microsoft's acquisition of Activision Blizzard, also weighed in, accusing Google of "using its search monopoly to coerce schools into adopting AI."

"Multiple courts have found Google liable for violating antitrust laws, and this latest move shows it remains unchastened," Khan wrote on X. "As the public increasingly pushes back against the use of AI in schools, Google is making clear it will punish those who don't fall in line."

New York City mayor Zohran Mamdani, a longstanding critic of oligopolies and corporate greed, said that "Google is trying to use its monopoly power to force AI into our public schools."

Google is trying to use its monopoly power to force AI into our public schools. No corporation should have the power to dictate how we teach our children.  Our public schools exist to serve students, not to grow the profit margins of Big Tech.

As NBC noted, research on the impact of AI in education is still quite limited, and some studies purporting to show meaningful benefits have been found to be flawed. That makes the headlong rush into AI integration, championed by massive AI companies and pretty much no one else, so controversial and potentially dangerous. Trying to force AI into classrooms while the technology's impact on mental health is an increasingly serious concern is audacious, and promises of more rigorous safety standards tend to ring hollow anyway: In October, the attorneys general for eight US states accused competing product OpenAI of ignoring its own safety warnings in pursuit of profit.

Google's new policy on AI in schools comes just days after shares in Google parent company Alphabet took a hammering as the company's AI Mode cut deep into its search business and revenue. I've reached out to Google for more information about the new educational policy and will update if I receive a reply.
